About 14 months ago, his wife developed mobility issues after a minor stroke, and Antonio took on managing her appointments, medications, and insurance paperwork in addition to coordinating the neighborhood garden and youth storytelling projects. The increased load of schedules, forms, and follow-through exposed longstanding but previously manageable organizational difficulties, leading to repeated missed appointments, double-booked commitments, and mounting tension at home when his wife felt she could not rely on him for practical tasks despite his obvious devotion. After a frustrated primary care visit where Antonio admitted he felt \"scattered\" and overwhelmed by basic daily responsibilities despite being otherwise physically well, his doctor referred him for a psychiatric evaluation to explore attention and planning problems that had become clearly impairing in his caregiving and community roles.\n- Chief Complaint: I keep forgetting everyday things like appointments and errands, and it’s starting to really mess up taking care of my wife and my commitments in the garden project.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): When I’m filling out the online forms for my wife’s insurance or the sign-up sheets for the garden project, I’ll swear I’ve done them carefully, but then I find out I skipped whole sections or typed the wrong date or medication dose because my eyes just kind of jumped over the small print.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start out meaning to sort my wife’s pills for the week or finish a new story for the kids’ group, but halfway through I wander off to stir a sauce or check on the tomatoes and then realize hours later that I never actually finished the first thing.\n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m constantly hunting for my glasses, the little notebook with all the appointment times, or the folder with the insurance papers, even though I just had them in my hand a minute before and put them down “somewhere safe” that I can’t remember.\n- S008 (Distractibility): When I try to sit down and call the hospital or go over the mail, the slightest thing—a bird at the feeder, the TV in the other room, or a thought about a recipe I want to try—pulls my mind away, and five minutes later I’m doing something else and the papers are still untouched.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Lately I’ll tell myself, “Remember, you have to call the physio at three and pick up her prescription after,” and then I get busy with the garden or talking to a neighbor and only realize I’ve completely forgotten until my wife asks me hours later what happened.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit in the waiting room at the clinic or stay beside my wife while she watches her shows, I feel this jumpy energy in my body, like I need to get up and move or fiddle with something, and I keep shifting in my seat and looking for excuses to stand up.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): Even when I try to relax with one of my old hobbies, like reading or painting quietly, I end up talking to myself, humming, getting up to check on something in the kitchen, or turning on the radio because just sitting there calmly in silence makes me antsy.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): From the moment I wake up, I’m bouncing between making breakfast, checking the garden, tidying the living room, and sorting mail so much that my daughter jokes I never stop moving, but I still don’t feel like I actually finish much of anything.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings with the garden committee or when the doctor is explaining something, I find myself jumping in with what I think they’re going to say or answering before they finish the question, and then I realize I’ve cut them off or missed an important detail.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m in line at the pharmacy or waiting for our turn at the clinic, I get impatient and fidgety, checking the time over and over, muttering about how long it’s taking, and sometimes I edge forward or try to ask the staff questions before it’s really my turn.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in primary school, my teachers were always telling my parents that I couldn’t sit still, was constantly talking out of turn, and left half-finished worksheets on my desk because I’d lose track of what we were supposed to be doing.\n- functional_impairment: These days my forgetfulness and jumping between tasks mean my wife sometimes misses or is late to appointments, our bills and insurance forms get messed up, and I’ve had to back out of parts of the garden and storytelling projects because people feel they can’t count on me to follow through.\n- duration: The way these attention and restlessness problems have really started to interfere with caring for my wife and keeping up with all the paperwork has been going on The increased load of schedules, forms, and follow-through exposed longstanding but previously manageable organizational difficulties, leading to repeated missed appointments, double-booked commitments, and mounting tension at home when his wife felt she could not rely on him for practical tasks despite his obvious devotion. After a frustrated primary care visit where Antonio admitted he felt \"scattered\" and overwhelmed by basic daily responsibilities despite being otherwise physically well, his doctor referred him for sort my wife’s pills for the week or finish a new story for the kids’ group, but halfway through I wander off to stir a sauce or check on the tomatoes and then realize hours later that I never actually finished the first thing.\n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m constantly hunting for my glasses, the little notebook with all the appointment times, or the folder with the insurance papers, even though I just had them in my hand a minute before and put them down “somewhere safe” that I can’t remember.\n- S008 (Distractibility): When I try to sit down and call the hospital or go over the mail, the slightest thing—a bird at the feeder, the TV in the other room, or a thought about a recipe I want to try—pulls my mind away, and five minutes later I’m doing something else and the papers are still untouched.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Lately I’ll tell myself, “Remember, you have to call the physio at three and pick up her prescription after,” and then I get busy with the garden or talking to a neighbor and only realize I’ve completely forgotten until my wife asks me hours later what happened.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit in the waiting room at the clinic or stay beside my wife while she watches her shows, I feel this jumpy energy in my body, like I need to get up and move or fiddle with something, and I keep shifting in my seat and looking for excuses to stand up.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): Even when I try to relax with one of my old hobbies, like reading or painting quietly, I end up talking to myself, humming, getting up to check on something in the kitchen, or turning on the radio because just sitting there calmly in silence makes me antsy.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): From the moment I wake up, I’m bouncing between making breakfast, checking the garden, tidying the living room, and sorting mail so much that my daughter jokes I never stop moving, but I still don’t feel like I actually finish much of anything.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings with the garden committee or when the doctor is explaining something, I find myself jumping in with what I think they’re going to say or answering before they finish the question, and then I realize I’ve cut them off or missed an important detail.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m in line at the pharmacy or waiting for our turn at the clinic, I get impatient and fidgety, checking the time over and over, muttering about how long it’s taking, and sometimes I edge forward or try to ask the staff questions before it’s really my turn.\n▸ S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings with the garden committee or when the doctor is explaining something, I find myself jumping in with what I think they’re going to say or answering before they finish the question, and then I realize I’ve cut them off or missed an important detail.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m in line at the pharmacy or waiting for our turn at the clinic, I Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nTo be honest, my own affairs are suffering just as much.
